Tautological relations and integrable systems
Alexandr Buryak, Sergey Shadrin

Abstract




Abstract.
We present a family of conjectural relations in the tautological cohomology of the moduli spaces of stable algebraic curves of genus
g
with
n
marked points. A large part of these relations has a surprisingly simple form: the tautological classes involved in the relations are given by stable graphs that are trees and that are decorated only by powers of the psi-classes at half-edges. We show that the proposed conjectural relations imply certain fundamental properties of the Dubrovin–Zhang (DZ) and the double ramification (DR) hierarchies associated to F-cohomological field theories. Our relations naturally extend a similar system of conjectural relations, which were proposed in an earlier work of the first author together with Guéré and Rossi and which are responsible for the normal Miura equivalence of the DZ and the DR hierarchy associated to an arbitrary cohomological field theory. Finally, we prove all of the above-mentioned relations in the case
n=1
and arbitrary
g
using a variation of the method from a paper by Liu and Pandharipande; this can be of independent interest. In particular, this proves the main conjecture from our previous joint work together with Hernández Iglesias. We also prove all of the above-mentioned relations in the case
g=0
and arbitrary
n
.

Keywords.
Moduli of curves, tautological ring, double ramification cycle, cohomological field theories, Dubrovin–Zhang hierarchy, double ramification hierarchy

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification.
14H10, 37K10, 53D45

1. Introduction

A remarkable relation between the geometry of the moduli spaces
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
of stable algebraic curves of genus
g
with
n
marked points and integrable systems has been an object of intensive research during more than 30 years. This relation was first manifested by Witten’s conjecture, see
[
Wit91
]
, proved by Kontsevich, see
[
Kon92
]
, saying that the generating series of the integrals

(1.1)

\displaystyle\intop\nolimits_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}}\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{i}^{d_{i}},\quad g,n\geq 0,\,\,d_{i}\geq 0,

where
\psi_{i}\in H^{2}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n},\mathbb C)
,
1\leq i\leq n
, is the first Chern class of the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
cotangent line bundle over
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
, gives a solution of the Korteweg–de Vries (KdV) hierarchy. It was then realized by Dubrovin and Zhang, see
[
DZ01
]
, that integrable systems appear in a much more general context where the central role is played by the notion of a
cohomological field theory
(CohFT), introduced by Kontsevich and Manin; see
[
KM94
]
. A cohomological field theory is a family of cohomology classes on the moduli spaces
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
, depending also on a vector in the
n^{\mathrm{th}}
tensor power of a fixed vector space
V
, that satisfy certain compatibility properties with respect to natural maps between different moduli spaces. Given a CohFT, Dubrovin and Zhang constructed
N:=\dim V
generating series
w^{\mathrm{top};\alpha}
,
1\leq\alpha\leq N
, by inserting cohomology classes forming the CohFT in the integrals (
1.1
) and proved that the resulting
N
-tuple
\overline{w}^{\,\mathrm{top}}:=(w^{\mathrm{top};1},\ldots,w^{\mathrm{top};N})
is a solution of an integrable hierarchy (of evolutionary PDEs with one spatial variable) canonically associated to our CohFT. This integrable hierarchy is called the
Dubrovin–Zhang
(
DZ
)
hierarchy
, and the solution
\overline{w}^{\,\mathrm{top}}
is called the
topological solution
. However, certain fundamental properties of this hierarchy, including the polynomiality of the equations, were left as an open problem. The polynomiality of the equations of the hierarchy was proved in
[
BPS12
]
for semisimple CohFTs (together with the polynomiality of the Hamiltonian structure, which we do not discuss in our paper). However, a satisfactory formula for the equations of the hierarchy was not found.

In
[
Bur15
]
, the first author suggested a new construction of an integrable system associated to an arbitrary CohFT. The construction uses certain cohomology classes on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
called the
double ramification
(
DR
)
cycles
, and so the new hierarchy was called the
DR hierarchy
. In contrast to the DZ hierarchy, the equations of the DR hierarchy are polynomial by construction, with a relatively simple formula for the coefficients given in terms of the intersection numbers of cohomology classes forming the CohFT and basic cohomology classes on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
including the DR cycles and the classes
\psi_{i}
. However, it is not known how to single out a solution of the DR hierarchy that has such a simple geometric interpretation as the topological solution of the DZ hierarchy. There is a choice of a solution that is natural for other reasons; see
[
Bur15
,
BDGR18
]
.

In
[
Bur15
]
, the first author conjectured that for an arbitrary CohFT, the DR and the DZ hierarchies are Miura equivalent, and in
[
BDGR18
]
, this conjecture was made more precise, giving a precise description of the required Miura transformation in terms of the partition function of the CohFT. In
[
BGR19
]
, the authors presented a family of relations in the cohomology of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
implying the Miura equivalence of the DR and the DZ hierarchies.

In
[
BR21
,
ABLR21
]
, the authors extended the construction of the DR hierarchy to objects that are much more general than CohFTs, the so-called
F-CohFTs
, introduced in
[
BR21
]
. Regarding the DZ hierarchies, their generalization for F-CohFTs was not considered in the literature before. In this paper, following the approach from
[
BPS12
]
, we show that there is a natural generalization of the DZ hierarchies for F-CohFTs.

In this paper, for any
m\geq 0
, we present a family of conjectural relations in the cohomology of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+m}
parameterized by integers
d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}\geq 0
satisfying
\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}\geq 2g+m-1
. For fixed
g,n,m,d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}
, our conjectural relation lies in
H^{2\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+m},\mathbb C)
. We explain that these conjectural relations naturally imply the following fundamental properties of the DR and the DZ hierarchies:

•

For
m\geq 2
, the relations imply the polynomiality of the DZ hierarchy associated to an arbitrary F-CohFT (Theorem
4.7
).

•

For
m=1
, the relations imply that the DR and the DZ hierarchies associated to an arbitrary F-CohFT are related by a Miura transformation (Theorem
4.10
). For
n=1
, the relation already appeared in
[
BHIS22
]
and proved to be true in the Gorenstein quotient of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,2}
in
[
Gub22
]
.

•

For
m=0
, the relations already appeared in
[
BGR19
]
. According to
[
DZ01
,
BDGR18
]
, the DR and the DZ hierarchies associated to an arbitrary CohFT are endowed with an additional structure, called a
tau-structure
. There are Miura transformations that preserve a tau-structure; they are called
normal Miura transformations
. By a result from
[
BGR19
]
, for
m=0
the relations imply that the DR and the DZ hierarchies associated to an arbitrary CohFT are related by a normal Miura transformation (see Section
4.4.4
).

We can thus view our family of conjectural relations as a natural extension of the family of relations presented in
[
BGR19
]
.

Example 1.1
.

Let us present our relations in the case
n=1
, leaving the general case to Section
2
. For any
k\geq 1
and
m,g,d\geq 0
, let us introduce the following set:

\mathcal{S}^{m,k}_{g,d}\coloneqq\left\{(\overline{g},\overline{d})\in\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}^{k}\times\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}^{k}\;\left|\;\begin{minipage}[256.0748pt]\small$d_{1}+\cdots+d_{k}+k-1=d$,\\
$g_{1}\geq\delta_{m\leq 1}$, $g_{2},\dots,g_{k}\geq 1$, $g_{1}+\cdots+g_{k}=g$,\\
$d_{1}+\cdots+d_{i}+i-1\leq 2(g_{1}+\cdots+g_{i})-2+m$ for any $i=1,\ldots,k-1$
\end{minipage}\right.\right\},

where
\overline{g}=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{k})
and
\overline{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{k})
.

•

Suppose
m\geq 2
. Our relations form a family of cohomological relations in
H^{2d}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,m+1},\mathbb C)
, for any
d\geq 2g-1+m
, and are given by

where we use the standard way to represent tautological cohomology classes using decorated stable graphs (see the details in Section

).

•

Suppose
m=1
. Our relations form a family of cohomological relations in
H^{2d}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,2},\mathbb C)
, for any
d\geq 2g
, and are given by

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{k\geq 1}(-1)^{k-1}\sumop\displaylimits_{(\overline{g},\overline{d})\in\mathcal{S}^{1,k}_{g,d}}\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern \displaystyle\;=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1},\ldots,g_{l}\geq 1\\

where
l=d-2g+1
,
\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{{{{\hbox{\framed@@{0.0pt}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{{\hbox{\kern 21.0pt\blacked@@}}{\hbox{\kern 30.92847pt\blacked@@}}}}\ignorespaces}}\hbox{\kern 20.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-1.18056pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{g}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{{}{}{}{}{}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{{}{}{}{}{}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 48.12845pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\coloneqq\lambda_{g}\mathrm{DR}_{g}(1,-1)
,
\mathrm{DR}_{g}(1,-1)
denotes the double ramification cycle, and
\lambda_{g}
is the top Chern class of the Hodge bundle over
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
.

•

Suppose
m=0
. Our relations form a family of cohomological relations in
H^{2d}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,1},\mathbb C)
, for any
d\geq 2g-1
, and are given by

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{k\geq 1}(-1)^{k-1}\sumop\displaylimits_{(\overline{g},\overline{d})\in\mathcal{S}^{0,k}_{g,d}}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.8073pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{{{\hbox{\ellipsed@{7.80728pt}{6.12498pt}}}}\hbox{\kern-7.8073pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-1.18056pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{g_{1}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{{}{}{}{}}}{{{}{}{}{}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.15111pt\raise 6.92557pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.56447pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\psi^{d_{1}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{{{\hbox{\ellipsed@{7.80728pt}{6.12498pt}}}}\hbox{\kern 32.8073pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-1.18056pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{g_{2}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{{}{}{}{}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.7657pt\raise 6.92557pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.56447pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\psi^{d_{2}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 73.42189pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots}$}}}}}}}{{{\hbox{\ellipsed@{11.3646pt}{6.12498pt}}}}\hbox{\kern 111.92189pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-1.18056pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{g_{k-1}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{{}{}{}{}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{{}{}{}{}}}{{{}{}{}{}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 130.83833pt\raise 6.92557pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.56447pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\psi^{d_{k-1}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{{{\hbox{\ellipsed@{8.0243pt}{6.12498pt}}}}\hbox{\kern 159.65111pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-1.18056pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{g_{k}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 200.90112pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}_{\,1}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{{}{}{}{}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 171.79552pt\raise 6.92557pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.56447pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\psi^{d_{k}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 200.69972pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

\displaystyle\;=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}g_{1},\ldots,g_{l}\geq 1\\
\sumop\displaylimits g_{i}=g\end{subarray}}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{l-1}\frac{g_{i}}{g_{i}+g_{i+1}+\cdots+g_{l}}\right)\pi_{*}\left(\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{{{{\hbox{\framed@@{0.0pt}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{{\hbox{\kern 29.0pt\blacked@@}}{\hbox{\kern 43.4146pt\blacked@@}}}}\ignorespaces}}\hbox{\kern 28.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-1.18056pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{g_{1}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-2.7986pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}_{2\,}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{{}{}{}{}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{{}{}{}{}}}{{{}{}{}{}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{{{{\hbox{\framed@@{0.0pt}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{{\hbox{\kern 69.6146pt\blacked@@}}{\hbox{\kern 84.02919pt\blacked@@}}}}\ignorespaces}}\hbox{\kern 68.6146pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-1.18056pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{g_{2}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{{}{}{}{}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 109.22919pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots}$}}}}}}}{{{{\hbox{\framed@@{0.0pt}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{{\hbox{\kern 148.72919pt\blacked@@}}{\hbox{\kern 161.72711pt\blacked@@}}}}\ignorespaces}}\hbox{\kern 147.72919pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-1.18056pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{g_{l}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{{}{}{}{}{}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 187.1285pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}_{\,1}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{{}{}{}{}{}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 186.9271pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\right),

where
l=d-2g+2
and the map
\pi\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,2}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,1}
forgets the second marked point.

We then prove all our relations in the case
n=1
and arbitrary
g
(Theorem
2.2
) using the method for constructing relations in
H^{*}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n},\mathbb C)
from the paper
[
LP11
]
. In particular, this proves the main conjecture from
[
BHIS22
]
and the conjectural relations from
[
BGR19
]
in the case
n=1
. We also prove all our relations in the case
g=0
and arbitrary
n
(Theorem
2.3
).

Finally, we fill a gap in the understanding of the equations of the DZ hierarchy mentioned above. An equation of the DZ hierarchy associated to an F-CohFT is the sum of the polynomial part and the fractional part (which conjecturally vanishes). We present a geometric formula for the polynomial part. The formula expresses the coefficient of a monomial in the polynomial part as the intersection of some universal cohomology class on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
with an element of the F-CohFT. Since the polynomiality of the equations of the DZ hierarchy is proved for semisimple CohFTs, this gives a geometric formula for the equations of the DZ hierarchy in this case.

Organization of the paper

Our conjectural relations are presented in Section
2
. For the case
m\geq 2
, this is formulated in Conjecture
1
, for the case
m=1
in Conjecture
2
, and for the case
m=0
in Conjecture
3
. As we already explained, Conjecture
3
was first proposed in
[
BGR19
]
. Then in Section
3
, we present an alternative formula for classes
B^{m}_{g,(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})}
appearing in the conjectures (Theorem
3.10
) and a particularly elegant reformulation of Conjecture
1
(Theorem
3.4
). In Section
4
, we explain the role of our conjectures in the study of integrable systems associated to cohomological field theories and F-cohomological field theories (see Theorems
4.7
and
4.10
and Section
4.4.4
). This section is independent of the other sections; a reader who is interested only in the geometrical part of our results can skip it. In Section
5
, we prove the conjectures in the case
n=1
, arbitrary
g
. In Section
6
, we prove the conjectures in the case
g=0
, arbitrary
n
. In Section
7
, we prove that the whole system of conjectural relations for
m\geq 2
(
i.e.
, Conjecture
1
) follows from its subsystem with
\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}=2g+m-1
and
d_{i}\geq 1
(Theorem
7.1
). Finally, in the appendix, we review a localization formula for the moduli space of stable relative maps to
(\mathbb P^{1},\infty)
, which is the main tool for our proof of the conjectures in the case
n=1
.

Notation and conventions

•

We denote by
H^{i}(X)
the cohomology groups of a topological space
X
with coefficients in
\mathbb C
. Let
H^{\mathrm{even}}(X):=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i\geq 0}H^{2i}(X)
.

•

Let
\llbracket n\rrbracket\coloneqq\{1,\dots,n\}
. Given a map
\llbracket n\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
i\mapsto d_{i}
, and a subset
I\subseteq\llbracket n\rrbracket
, let
d_{I}
denote
\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}d_{i}
(in particular,
d_{\emptyset}=0
and
d_{\{i\}}=d_{i}
).

•

Let
(a)_{n}
denote the Pochhammer symbol
(a)_{n}\coloneqq\Gamma(a+1)/\Gamma(a+1-n)
. In particular,
(a)_{0}=1
and
(a)_{n}=a(a-1)\cdots(a-n+1)
for
n\geq 1
.

•

We use the standard convention for sums over repeated Greek indices.

•

We will work with the moduli spaces
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
of stable algebraic curves of genus
g
with
n
marked points, which are defined for
g,n\geq 0
satisfying the condition
2g-2+n>0
. We will often omit mentioning this condition explicitly and silently assume that it is satisfied when a moduli space is considered.

2. Conjectural cohomological relations

2.1. Tautological cohomology of
\boldsymbol{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}}

Let us recall briefly the standard notation concerning tautological cohomology classes on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
, referring a reader to
[
PPZ15
, Sections 0.2 and 0.3]
for more details.

We use the standard cohomology classes on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
:

•

The
i^{\mathrm{th}}

psi class

\psi_{i}\in H^{2}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})
,
1\leq i\leq n
, is the first Chern class of the cotangent line bundle
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
whose fibers are the cotangent spaces at the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
marked point on stable curves.

•

The
i^{\mathrm{th}}

kappa class

\kappa_{i}\in H^{2i}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})
,
i\geq 0
, is defined as
\kappa_{i}:=\pi_{*}(\psi_{n+1}^{i+1})
, where
\pi\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+1}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
is the map forgetting the last marked point.

•

The
i^{\mathrm{th}}

Hodge class

\lambda_{i}\in H^{2i}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})
,
i\geq 0
, is the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
Chern class of the Hodge vector bundle
\mathbb{E}_{g}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
whose fibers are the spaces of holomorphic differentials on stable curves.

We denote by
G_{g,n}
the set of stable graphs of genus
g
with
n
legs marked by numbers
1,\ldots,n
. For a stable graph
\Gamma
, we use the following notation:

•

V(\Gamma)
,
E(\Gamma)
,
H(\Gamma)
, and
L(\Gamma)
are the sets of vertices, edges, half-edges, and legs of
\Gamma
, respectively.

•

The leg of
\Gamma
marked by
1\leq i\leq|L(\Gamma)|
is denoted by
\sigma_{i}
.

•

For
h\in H(\Gamma)
, let
v(h)
be the vertex incident to
h
.

•

Set
H^{e}(\Gamma):=H(\Gamma)\backslash L(\Gamma)
, and for
h\in H^{e}(\Gamma)
, we denote by
\iota(h)
a unique half-edge that together with
h
forms an edge of
\Gamma
.

•

For
v\in V(\Gamma)
, let

–

g(v)
be the genus of
v
,

–

n(v)
be the degree of
v
,

–

H[v]
be the set of half-edges incident to
v
,

–

r(v):=2g(v)-2+n(v)
.

Consider a stable graph
\Gamma
.

•

We associate to
\Gamma
the space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}:=\prodop\displaylimits_{v\in V(\Gamma)}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g(v),n(v)}
.

•

There is a canonical morphism
\xi_{\Gamma}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g(\Gamma),|L(\Gamma)|}
. Here
g(\Gamma)\coloneqq b_{1}(\Gamma)+\sumop\displaylimits_{v\in V(\Gamma)}g(v)
, where
b_{1}(\Gamma)
is the first Betti number of
\Gamma
.

•

A
decoration
on
\Gamma
is a choice of numbers
x_{i}[v],y[h]\geq 0
,
i\geq 1
,
v\in V(\Gamma)
,
h\in H(\Gamma)
. Given such numbers, we say that we have a
decorated stable graph
.

•

To a decorated stable graph, we associate the cohomology classes

\gamma:=\prodop\displaylimits_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\prodop\displaylimits_{i\geq 1}\kappa_{i}[v]^{x_{i}[v]}\cdot\prodop\displaylimits_{h\in H(\Gamma)}\psi_{h}^{y[h]}\in H^{*}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma})\quad\text{and}\quad\xi_{\Gamma*}(\gamma)\in H^{*}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g(\Gamma),|L(\Gamma)|}),

where
\kappa_{i}[v]
is the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
kappa class on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g(v),n(v)}
and
\psi_{h}
is a psi class on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g(v(h)),n(v(h))}
. The class
\xi_{\Gamma*}(\gamma)
is called a
basic tautological class
on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g(\Gamma),|L(\Gamma)|}
. We will often denote it by a picture of the decorated stable graph.

Denote by
R^{*}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})
the subspace of
H^{*}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})
spanned by all basic tautological classes. The subspace
R^{*}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})\subset H^{*}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})
is closed under multiplication and is called the
tautological ring
of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
. Let
R^{i}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}):=R^{*}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})\cap H^{2i}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})
. Linear relations between basic tautological classes are called
tautological relations
.

The Hodge classes on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
are tautological;
\lambda_{i}\in R^{i}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})
.

Recall the string equation, see
[
Wit91
]
, in the following form. Let
\pi\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,k+q+1}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,k}
be the projection forgetting the last
q+1
marked points. Then

(2.1)

\displaystyle\pi_{*}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{k}\psi_{i}^{q_{i}}\right)=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq p_{i}\leq q_{i},\,i\in\llbracket k\rrbracket\\
q_{\llbracket k\rrbracket}-p_{\llbracket k\rrbracket}=q+1\end{subarray}}\frac{(q+1)!}{\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{k}(q_{i}-p_{i})!}\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{k}\psi_{i}^{p_{i}}.

For
A=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\in\mathbb Z^{n}
,
\sumop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=0
, let
\mathrm{DR}_{g}(A)\in H^{2g}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})
be the
double ramification
(
DR
)
cycle
. Let us briefly recall the definition. The positive
a_{i}
define a partition
\mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{l(\mu)})
, and the negative
a_{i}
define a second partition
\nu=(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{l(\nu)})
of the same size. Let
n_{0}:=n-l(\mu)-l(\nu)
, and consider the moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n_{0}}(\mathbb P^{1},\mu,\nu)^{\sim}
of stable relative maps to rubber
\mathbb P^{1}
with ramification profiles
\mu,\nu
over the points
0,\infty\in\mathbb P^{1}
, respectively. Then the double ramification cycle
\mathrm{DR}_{g}(A)
is defined as the Poincaré dual to the pushforward of the virtual fundamental class
\left[{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n_{0}}(\mathbb P^{1},\mu,\nu)^{\sim}\right]^{\mathrm{vir}}
to
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
via the forgetful map
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n_{0}}(\mathbb P^{1},\mu,\nu)^{\sim}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
.

Abusing notation, for
A=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathbb Z^{n}
and
B=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{m})\in\mathbb Z^{m}
satisfying
\sumop\displaylimits a_{i}+\sumop\displaylimits b_{j}=0
, we denote by
\mathrm{DR}_{g}(A,B)
the double ramification cycle
\mathrm{DR}_{g}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},b_{1},\ldots,b_{m})
.

We have
\mathrm{DR}_{g}(A)\in R^{g}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})
(see,
e.g.
,
[
JPPZ17
]
). The restriction
\left.\mathrm{DR}_{g}(A)\right|_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathrm{ct}}_{g,n}}\in H^{2g}({\mathcal{M}}^{\mathrm{ct}}_{g,n})
depends polynomially on the
a_{i}
, where by
{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathrm{ct}}_{g,n}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
we denote the moduli space of curves of compact type. This implies that the class
\lambda_{g}\mathrm{DR}_{g}(A)\in R^{2g}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})
depends polynomially on the
a_{i}
. Moreover, the resulting polynomial (with the coefficients in
R^{2g}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})
) is homogeneous of degree
2g
. There is also the following property that we will need. If
g\geq 1
and
\pi\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+1}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
is the map forgetting the last marked point, then, see
[
BDGR18
, Lemma 5.1]
,

(2.2)

\displaystyle\text{the polynomial class $\pi_{*}\left(\lambda_{g}\mathrm{DR}_{g}\left(-\sumop\displaylimits a_{i},a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\right)\right)\in R^{2g}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})$ is divisible by $a_{n}^{2}$}.

The polynomiality of the class
\mathrm{DR}_{g}(A)\in R^{g}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})
has been proved by A. Pixton and D. Zagier (we thank A. Pixton for informing us about that), but the proof is not published yet.

2.2. Preliminary combinatorial definitions

By a
stable tree
, we mean a stable graph
\Gamma
with the first Betti number
b_{1}(\Gamma)
equal to zero. A
stable rooted tree
is a stable tree together with a choice of a vertex
v\in V(T)
called the
root
.

Consider a stable rooted tree
T
.

•

We denote by
H_{+}(T)
the set of half-edges of
T
that are directed away from the root. Clearly,
L(T)\subset H_{+}(T)
. Let
H^{e}_{+}(T):=H_{+}(T)\backslash L(T)
and
H^{e}_{-}(T):=H^{e}(T)\backslash H^{e}_{+}(T)
.

•

A
path
in
T
is a sequence of pairwise distinct vertices
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\in V(T)
such that for any
1\leq i\leq k-1
, the vertices
v_{i}
and
v_{i+1}
are connected by an edge.

•

A vertex
w\in V(T)
is called a
descendant
of a vertex
v\in V(T)
if
v
is on the unique path from the root to
w
. Denote by
\mathrm{Desc}[v]
the set of all descendants of
v
. Note that
v\in\mathrm{Desc}[v]
.

•

A vertex
w
is called a
direct descendant
of
v
if
w\in\mathrm{Desc}[v]
,
w\neq v
, and
w
and
v
are connected by an edge. In this case, the vertex
v
is called the
mother
of
w
.

•

For two half-edges
h_{1},h_{2}\in H_{+}(T)
, we say that
h_{2}
is a
descendant
of
h_{1}
if
h_{1}=h_{2}
or
v(h_{2})\in\mathrm{Desc}[v(\iota(h_{1}))]
.

•

A function
l\colon V(T)\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 1}
is called a
level function
if the following conditions are satisfied:

a)

The value of
l
on the root is equal to
1
.

b)

If a vertex
v
is the mother of a vertex
v^{\prime}
, then
l(v^{\prime})>l(v)
.

c)

For every
1\leq i\leq\deg(l)
, the set
l^{-1}(i)
is nonempty, where
\deg(l):=\max_{v\in V(T)}l(v)
.

•

There is a natural level function
l_{T}\colon V(T)\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 1}
uniquely determined by the condition that if a vertex
v
is the mother of a vertex
v^{\prime}
, then
l_{T}(v^{\prime})=l_{T}(v)+1
. We call this level function
canonical
. The number
\deg(T):=\deg(l_{T})
is called the
degree
of
T
.

•

For a level function
l\colon V(T)\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 1}
, it is convenient to extend it to
H_{+}(T)
by taking
l(h):=k
if the half-edge
h
is attached to a vertex of level
k
.

•

For
k\geq 1
, we set
g_{k}(T):=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in V(T)\\
l_{T}(v)\leq k\end{subarray}}g(v)
.

Let
m\geq 0
and
n\geq 1
. Let us consider stable rooted trees
T
with at least
n+m
legs, where we split the set of legs
L(T)=\{\sigma_{i}\}
into three subsets:

a)

The legs
\sigma_{1},\dotsc,\sigma_{n}
are called the
regular legs
.

b)

The legs
\sigma_{n+1},\ldots,\sigma_{n+m}
are called the
frozen legs
; we require that they are attached to the root.

c)

Any
extra legs
, whose set is denoted by
F(T)
, correspond to additional marked points that we will eventually forget.

The set of such trees will be denoted by
\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m;\circ}
. We will also use the following notation:

\displaystyle\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m}:=\left\{T\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m;\circ}\;|\;F(T)=\emptyset\right\}\subset\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m;\circ},

\displaystyle\mathrm{SRT}^{k}_{g,n,m;\circ}:=\left\{T\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m;\circ}\;|\;|V(T)|=k\right\}\subset\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m;\circ}.

Consider a tree
T\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m;\circ}
.

•

A vertex of
T
is called
potentially unstable
if it becomes unstable once we forget all of the extra legs.

•

Let

H^{em}_{+}(T):=H^{e}_{+}(T)\sqcup\{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n+m}\},\quad\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T):=H^{e}_{+}(T)\sqcup\{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n}\}.

•

For
h\in\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)
, define
I_{h}:=\{1\leq i\leq n\;|\;\text{$\sigma_{i}$ is a descendant of $h$}\}\subseteq\llbracket n\rrbracket
.

A tree
T\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m;\circ}
is called
balanced
if

a)

there are no extra legs attached to the root;

b)

for every vertex except the root, there is at least one extra leg attached to it.

The set of all balanced trees will be denoted by
\mathrm{SRT}^{(b)}_{g,n,m;\circ}\subset\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m;\circ}
.

For a balanced tree
T
, define a function
q\colon H^{e}_{+}(T)\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}
by requiring that for a half-edge
h\in H_{+}^{e}(T)
, there are exactly
q(h)+1
extra legs attached to the vertex
v=v(\iota(h))
. Given an
n
-tuple of nonnegative integers
\overline{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})
, we extend the function
q
to the set
\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)
by setting
q(\sigma_{i}):=d_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
.

We say that a balanced tree
T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
is
complete
if the following conditions are satisfied:

a)

Every vertex has at least one descendant
v\in V(T)
with
l_{T}(v)=\deg(T)
.

b)

We have
l_{T}(\sigma_{i})=\deg(T)
for
1\leq i\leq n
.

c)

Each vertex
v
with
l_{T}(v)=\deg(T)
is attached to at least one regular leg.

d)

For every
1\leq l\leq\deg(T)
, the set of vertices
l_{T}^{-1}(l)
contains at least one vertex that is not potentially unstable.

The set of all complete trees will be denoted by
\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c)}_{g,n,m;\circ}\subset\mathrm{SRT}^{(b)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
.

We say that a tree
T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
is
admissible
if for every
1\leq k<\deg(T)
, the following condition is satisfied:

(2.3)

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in H_{+}^{e}(T)\\
l_{T}(h)=k\end{subarray}}q(h)\leq 2g_{k}(T)-2+m.

The set of all admissible trees will be denoted by
\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c,a)}_{g,n,m;\circ}\subset\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
.

Note that the sets
\mathrm{SRT}^{(b)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
and
\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
are infinite except for a finite number of triples
(g,n,m)
. However, the set
\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c,a)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
is finite, which follows from condition (
2.3
).

2.3. Conjectural tautological relations

For a balanced tree
T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
and an
n
-tuple of nonnegative integers
\overline{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})
, define

[T,\overline{d}]:=\xi_{T*}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)}\psi_{h}^{q(h)}\right)\in R^{\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}+\#F(T)}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+m+\#F(T)}).

Consider the map

\mathrm{e}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+m+\#F(T)}\longrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+m}

forgetting all of the extra legs, and the class

\mathrm{e}_{*}[T,\overline{d}]\in R^{\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+m}).

Definition 2.1
.

For
m\geq 0
,
g\geq 0
,
n\geq 1
, and
\overline{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})\in\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}^{n}
, we define

B^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}:=\sumop\displaylimits_{T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c,a)}_{g,n,m;\circ}}(-1)^{\deg(T)-1}\mathrm{e}_{*}[T,\overline{d}]\in R^{\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+m}).

Note that

•

the class
B^{0}_{g,\overline{d}}
coincides with the class
B^{g}_{d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}}
from the paper
[
BGR19
]
,

•

the class
B^{1}_{g,2g}
coincides with the class
B^{g}
from the paper
[
BHIS22
]
.

We can now formulate our first conjecture.

Conjecture 1
.

For any
m\geq 2
,
g\geq 0
,
n\geq 1
, and
\overline{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})\in\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}^{n}
such that
\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}\geq 2g+m-1
, we have
B^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}=0
in
R^{\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+m})
.

In order to present our second conjecture, we need more definitions. Let
n,k\geq 1
. Consider a stable tree
T\in\mathrm{ST}^{k}_{g,n,1}
and integers
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1}
such that
a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n+1}=0
. There is a unique way to assign an integer
a(h)
to each half-edge
h\in H(T)
in such a way that the following conditions hold:

a)

For any leg
\sigma_{i}\in L(T)
, we have
a(\sigma_{i})=a_{i}
.

b)

If
h\in H^{e}(T)
, then
a(h)+a(\iota(h))=0
.

c)

For any vertex
v\in V(T)
, we have
\sumop\displaylimits_{h\in H[v]}a(h)=0
.

Consider the space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{T}=\prodop\displaylimits_{v\in V(T)}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g(v),n(v)}
. For each vertex
v\in V(T)
, the numbers
a(h)
,
h\in H[v]
, define the double ramification cycle
\mathrm{DR}_{g(v)}\left(A_{H[v]}\right)\in R^{g(v)}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g(v),n(v)})
, where
A_{H[v]}
is the tuple
(a(h_{1}),\ldots,a(h_{n(v)}))
, where
\{h_{1},\ldots,h_{n(v)}\}=H[v]
. If we multiply all of these DR cycles, we get the class

\prodop\displaylimits_{v\in V(T)}\mathrm{DR}_{g(v)}\left(A_{H[v]}\right)\in H^{2g}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{T}).

We define a class
\mathrm{DR}_{T}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\in R^{g+k-1}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+1})
by

\displaystyle\mathrm{DR}_{T}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1}):=\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{h\in H^{e}_{+}(T)}a(h)\right)\xi_{T*}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{v\in V(T)}\mathrm{DR}_{g(v)}\left(A_{H[v]}\right)\right).

After multiplication by
\lambda_{g}
, this class becomes a polynomial in
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1}
with coefficients in the space
R^{2g+k-1}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+1})
, which is homogeneous of degree
2g+k-1
. To the stable tree
T
, we also assign a number
C(T)
by setting

C(T):=\prodop\displaylimits_{v\in V(T)}\frac{r(v)}{\sumop\displaylimits_{\widetilde{v}\in\mathrm{Desc}[v]}r(\widetilde{v})}.

We introduce the following cohomology class:

\check{A}_{g}^{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1}):=\sumop\displaylimits_{T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{k}_{g,n,1}}C(T)\lambda_{g}\mathrm{DR}_{T}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1})\in R^{2g+k-1}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+1}),

depending on integers
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n+1}
with vanishing sum. The class

\check{A}_{g}^{k}\left(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},-\sumop\displaylimits a_{i}\right)

is a polynomial in
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
, homogeneous of degree
2g+k-1
. For an
n
-tuple of integers
\overline{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})\in\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}^{n}
satisfying
\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}\geq 2g
, define

\displaystyle A^{1}_{g,\overline{d}}:=\mathrm{Coef}_{a_{1}^{d_{1}}\cdots a_{n}^{d_{n}}}\check{A}_{g}^{\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}-2g+1}\left(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},-\sumop\displaylimits a_{i}\right)\in R^{\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+1}).

We can now present our second conjecture.

Conjecture 2
.

For any
g\geq 0
,
n\geq 1
, and an
n
-tuple of nonnegative integers
\overline{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})
with
\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}\geq 2g
, we have
B^{1}_{g,\overline{d}}=A^{1}_{g,\overline{d}}
in
R^{\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+1})
.

Note that the
n=1
case of this conjecture appeared in
[
BHIS22
]
, and in
[
Gub22
]
, the author proved that the relation
B^{1}_{g,d}=A^{1}_{g,d}
is true in the Gorenstein quotient of
R^{d}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,2})
.

Let us now recall the conjecture from
[
BGR19
]
, which together with our Conjectures
1
and
2
naturally forms a series of conjectures involving the classes
B^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}
for all
m\geq 0
. Let
n,k\geq 1
. Consider the map
\pi\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+1}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
forgetting the last marked point. By
[
BGR19
, Lemma 2.2]
, the class
\pi_{*}\check{A}_{g}^{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},-\sumop\displaylimits a_{i})
(as a polynomial in
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
) is divisible by
\sumop\displaylimits a_{i}
. Following the paper
[
BGR19
]
, we define

A_{g}^{k}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}):=\frac{1}{\sumop\displaylimits a_{i}}\pi_{*}\check{A}_{g}^{k}\left(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n},-\sumop\displaylimits a_{i}\right)\in R^{2g+k-2}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}),

which is a polynomial in
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
, homogeneous of degree
2g+k-2
, and then define

\displaystyle A^{0}_{g,\overline{d}}:=\mathrm{Coef}_{a_{1}^{d_{1}}\cdots a_{n}^{d_{n}}}A_{g}^{\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}-2g+2}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in R^{\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})

for any
n
-tuple
\overline{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})\in\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}^{n}
satisfying
\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}\geq 2g-1
.

Conjecture 3

(
cf.

[
BGR19
]
)
.

For any
g\geq 0
,
n\geq 1
, and an
n
-tuple of nonnegative integers
\overline{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})
with
\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}\geq 2g-1
, we have
B^{0}_{g,\overline{d}}=A^{0}_{g,\overline{d}}
in
R^{\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})
.

We can prove all of the above conjectures in the following cases.

Theorem 2.2
.

Conjectures
1
,
2
, and
3
are true for
n=1
.

Theorem 2.3
.

Conjectures
1
,
2
, and
3
are true for
g=0
.

The proofs will be presented in Sections
5
and
6
, respectively.

3. An equivalent formulation of the conjectures

3.1. The case
\boldsymbol{m\geq 2}

Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
be formal variables assigned to the legs
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}
. Recall that for
I\subset\llbracket n\rrbracket
we use the notation
x_{I}=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I}x_{i}
.

Definition 3.1
.

For
g,m\geq 0
and
n\geq 1
, define the following class in
R^{*}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+m})[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
:

(3.1)

\displaystyle P_{g,n,m}\coloneqq\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{-1}\sumop\displaylimits_{T\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m}}(-1)^{|E(T)|}\sumop\displaylimits_{p\colon\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}}\xi_{T*}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)}\psi_{h}^{p(h)}\right)\prodop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)}x_{I_{h}}^{p(h)+1}.

Note that given
T\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m}
and
p\colon\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}
, we have
\xi_{T*}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)}\psi_{h}^{p(h)}\right)=0
unless
\sumop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}[v]}p(h)\leq 3g(v)-3+n(v)
for each
v\in V(T)
. This implies that the second sum in the definition of
P_{g,n,m}
has a finite number of nonzero terms. Clearly, the coefficient of
x_{1}^{d_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{d_{n}}
in
P_{g,n,m}
is a tautological class from
R^{\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+m})
, and it is equal to the sum of
\psi_{1}^{d_{1}}\cdots\psi_{n}^{d_{n}}
and a class supported on the boundary of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+m}
.

Example 3.2
.

For instance, let
T\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,3,2}
be as in the following picture, with
g=g_{1}+g_{2}+g_{3}
(the root vertex is labeled by
g_{1}
, and hence the legs
\sigma_{4}
and
\sigma_{5}
are attached to it) and the values of the function
p\colon\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}
being
p_{1},\ldots,p_{5}
at the corresponding half-edges in the picture:

\displaystyle\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 22.8073pt\raise-28.99994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 48.6146pt\raise-28.99994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 74.42189pt\raise-28.99994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 100.22919pt\raise-28.99994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 126.03648pt\raise-28.99994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 151.84378pt\raise-28.99994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}}\,.

Then the class that this pair
(T,p)
contributes to
P_{g,3,2}
is multiplied by the factor

x_{1}^{p_{1}}x_{2}^{p_{2}+p_{5}+1}x_{3}^{p_{3}}(x_{1}+x_{2})^{p_{4}+1}.

Remark 3.3
.

Since in our notation we have
x_{\emptyset}=0
, this implies the following condition for a tree
T
to be able to contribute nontrivially to
P_{g,n,m}
. Namely, for each
h\in\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)
, there must exist at least one leg
\sigma_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, which is a descendant of
h
; that is,
I_{h}\neq\emptyset
. This means that any vertex of
T
that does not have direct descendants should have at least one leg
\sigma_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, attached to it.

Theorem 3.4
.

The following two statements are equivalent:

(1)

Conjecture
1
is true.

(2)

We have
\deg P_{g,n,m}\leq 2g+m-2
for all
g\geq 0
,
n\geq 1
, and
m\geq 2
.

Proof.

Let us introduce an auxiliary cohomology class on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+m}
. We will say that a tree
T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
is
nondegenerate
if it does not have potentially unstable vertices. The set of such trees will be denoted by
\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,nd)}_{g,n,m;\circ}\subset\mathrm{SRT}^{(b)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
. The set
\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,nd)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
is infinite, except for a finite number of triples
(g,n,m)
. However, we have the following statement.

Lemma 3.5
.

Given
\overline{d}\in\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}^{n}
, the set of trees
T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,nd)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
such that

[T,\overline{d}]\neq 0\in R^{\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}+|F(T)|}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+m+|F(T)|})

is finite.

Proof.

For
T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,nd)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
, denote by
\pi(T)\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m}
the stable tree obtained from
T
by forgetting all of the extra legs in
T
. Clearly, we can identify
V(T)=V(\pi(T))
. It is sufficient to prove that for any
\widetilde{T}\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m}
, there exists a constant
N_{\widetilde{T}}
such that if
\pi(T)=\widetilde{T}
and
[T,\overline{d}]\neq 0
for
T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,nd)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
, then
|F(T)|\leq N_{\widetilde{T}}
. Let us prove it by induction on
|V(\widetilde{T})|
. If
|V(\widetilde{T})|=1
, then we can obviously set
N_{\widetilde{T}}:=0
. Suppose
|V(\widetilde{T})|\geq 2
. Let us choose a vertex
v\in V(\widetilde{T})
that does not have direct descendants. Denote by
w
the mother of
v
and by
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}
the stable tree obtained from
\widetilde{T}
by deleting the vertex
v
together with all of the half-edges incident to it. Let us show that we can set

N_{\widetilde{T}}:=2N_{\widetilde{T}^{\prime}}+3g(w)-2+n+m+|E(\widetilde{T})|.

Indeed, denote by
h\in H^{e}_{+}[w]
the half-edge such that
v(\iota(h))=v
and by
T^{\prime}
the stable tree obtained from
T
by deleting the vertex
v
together with all of the half-edges incident to it. If
[T,\overline{d}]\neq 0
, then clearly

q(h)\leq 3g(w)-3+n(w)\leq 3g(w)-3+|E(\widetilde{T})|+n+m+|F(T^{\prime})|.

Since
q(h)=|F[v]|-1
, using the induction assumption, we see that
|F(T)|=|F(T^{\prime})|+|F[v]|\leq 2N_{\widetilde{T}^{\prime}}+3g(w)-2+n+m+|E(\widetilde{T})|
, as required.
∎

For
g,m\geq 0
,
n\geq 1
, and
d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}\geq 0
, let us consider the following cohomology class:

(3.2)

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}:=\sumop\displaylimits_{T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,nd)}_{g,n,m;\circ}}(-1)^{|E(T)|}\mathrm{e}_{*}[T,\overline{d}]\in R^{\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+m}).

Lemma
3.5
implies that this class is well defined.

Proposition 3.6
.

Conjecture
1
is true if and only if
\widetilde{B}^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}=0
for all
g\geq 0
,
n\geq 1
,
m\geq 2
, and
d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}\geq 0
such that
\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}>2g-2+m
.

Proof.

The proof consists of two steps.

Step
1. Let us prove that

(3.3)

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}=\sumop\displaylimits_{T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c)}_{g,n,m;\circ}}(-1)^{\deg(T)-1}\mathrm{e}_{*}[T,\overline{d}]\in R^{\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+m}),\quad\overline{d}\in\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}^{n}.

Consider a tree
T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
. It is clear that any potentially unstable vertex
v
in
T
does not coincide with the root and has genus
0
, and there is exactly one half-edge
h_{1}
from
\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T)
attached to it. Moreover, there is exactly one half-edge
h_{2}
from
H^{e}_{-}(T)
attached to it, and
\mathrm{e}_{*}[T,\overline{d}]=0
unless
q(h_{1})=q(\iota(h_{2}))
. Let us denote by
\widetilde{\mathrm{SRT}}^{(b)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
the set of trees
T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
such that the condition

(3.4)

\displaystyle q(h_{1})=q(\iota(h_{2}))

is satisfied for any potentially unstable vertex. Note that
h_{1}
in condition (
3.4
) can be a leg
\sigma_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, so the set
\widetilde{\mathrm{SRT}}^{(b)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
depends on the choice of
\overline{d}
.

Let us fix
\overline{d}
. Consider a tree
T\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SRT}}^{(b)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
and a potentially unstable vertex
v\in V(T)
. Let us throw away the vertex
v
together with all of the half-edges attached to it except
h_{1}
and identify the half-edges
h_{1}
and
\iota(h_{2})
. We obtain a tree from
\widetilde{\mathrm{SRT}}^{(b)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
with one less potentially unstable vertex. Repeating this procedure until there are no potentially unstable vertices, we obtain a tree from
\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,nd)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
, which we denote by
\mathrm{Core}(T)
. We obviously have

\mathrm{e}_{*}[T,\overline{d}]=\mathrm{e}_{*}[\mathrm{Core}(T),\overline{d}].

Therefore, Equation (
3.3
) is a corollary of the following lemma.

Lemma 3.7
.

For any
\overline{d}\in\mathbb Z^{n}_{\geq 0}
and
T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,nd)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
, we have
\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{T}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SRT}}^{(b,c)}_{g,n,m;\circ}\\
\mathrm{Core}(\widetilde{T})=T\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\deg(\widetilde{T})-1}=(-1)^{|E(T)|}
.

Proof.

The proof is by the induction on
|V(T)|
. The base case
|V(T)|=1
is obvious.

Suppose
|V(T)|>1
. Since
T=\mathrm{Core}(\widetilde{T})
is obtained from
\widetilde{T}
by deleting some vertices, we can consider the set
V(T)
as a subset of
V(\widetilde{T})
. Denote by
V^{l}(T)
the set of vertices
v\in V(T)
that do not have direct descendants. Consider a nonempty subset
I\subset V^{l}(T)
, and let

n_{I}:=|\{1\leq i\leq n\;|\;\text{$\sigma_{i}$ is attached to a vertex from $I$}\}|,\quad g_{I}:=\sumop\displaylimits_{v\in I}g(v).

Denote by
T^{\prime}
the tree obtained from
T
by deleting all of the vertices from
I
together with all of the half-edges attached to them, where we consider the half-edges
\iota(h)
, for
h\in H^{e}_{-}(T)
attached to the vertices from
I
, as regular legs of the tree
T^{\prime}
. Therefore,
T^{\prime}\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,nd)}_{g-g_{I},n-n_{I}+|I|,m;\circ}
. Denote by
S
the set of trees
\widetilde{T}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SRT}}^{(b,c)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
such that

\left\{\left.v\in V(\widetilde{T})\;\right|\;\text{$l_{T}(v)=\deg(\widetilde{T})$ and $v$ is not potentially unstable}\right\}=I.

Deleting all of the vertices
v
with
l_{T}(v)=\deg(T)
together with all of the half-edges attached to them gives a bijection from the set
S
to the set
\left\{\left.\widetilde{T}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SRT}}^{(b,c)}_{g-g_{I},n-n_{I}+|I|,m;\circ}\;\right|\;\mathrm{Core}(\widetilde{T}^{\prime})=T^{\prime}\right\}
. Using the induction assumption, we obtain

\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widetilde{T}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{SRT}}^{(b,c)}_{g,n,m;\circ}\\
\mathrm{Core}(\widetilde{T})=T\end{subarray}}\hskip-5.69046pt(-1)^{\deg(\widetilde{T})-1}=\hskip-2.84544pt\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\subset V^{l}(T)\\
I\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\hskip-2.84544pt(-1)^{|E(T)|-|I|+1}=(-1)^{|E(T)|+1}\sumop\displaylimits_{k=1}^{|V^{l}(T)|}(-1)^{k}{|V^{l}(T)|\choose k}=(-1)^{|E(T)|},

as required.
∎

Step
2. Let us now prove the proposition using Equation (
3.3
). For a tree
T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
and an
n
-tuple
\overline{d}
satisfying
\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}>2g-2+m
, denote by
l_{0}
the minimal level such that
\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)\\
l_{T}(h)=l_{0}\end{subarray}}q(h)>2g_{l_{0}}(T)-2+m
. Let us also introduce the following notation:

\displaystyle I_{0}:=\left\{\left.h\in\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)\;\right|\;l_{T}(h)=l_{0}\right\},\quad V_{0}:=\left\{\left.v\in V(T)\;\right|\;l_{T}(v)\leq l_{0}\right\},\quad g_{0}:=g_{l_{0}}(T),

and for
h\in I_{0}
, let

\displaystyle V_{h}:=\{v\in V(T)\;|\;\text{$v\in\mathrm{Desc}[v(\iota(h))]$}\},\quad g_{h}:=\sumop\displaylimits_{v\in V_{h}}g(v).

Denote by
T_{0}
the subtree of
T
formed by the vertices from
V_{0}
, and denote by
T_{h}
the subtree of
T
formed by the vertices from
V_{h}
,
h\in I_{0}
. We see that
T_{0}\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c,a)}_{g_{0},|I_{0}|,m;\circ}
and
T_{h}\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g_{h},|I_{h}|,1;\circ}
, where we consider
\iota(h)
as a unique frozen leg in
T_{h}
.

Suppose that Conjecture
1
is true. In the sum over
T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
on the right-hand side of (
3.3
), we can collect together the terms with fixed
g_{0}
,
|I_{0}|
, and trees
T_{h}
. Denote it by
S
, and let
\overline{d}^{\prime}
be an
|I_{0}|
-tuple consisting of the numbers
q(h)
,
h\in I_{0}
. There is a gluing map from a product of moduli space, determined by the decomposition of the tree
T
into the trees
T_{0}
and
T_{h}
,
h\in I_{0}
, to
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+m}
, and it is easy to see that the class
S
is equal to the pushforward under this gluing map of the tensor product of the class
B^{m}_{g_{0},\overline{d}^{\prime}}
and the classes determined by the trees
T_{h}
. Since we assumed that Conjecture
1
is true, we conclude that
S=0
, and thus also
\widetilde{B}^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}=0
.

Now suppose
\widetilde{B}^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}=0
for all
g\geq 0
,
n\geq 1
,
m\geq 2
, and
d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}\geq 0
such that
\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}>2g-2+m
. Let us prove that
B^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}=0
in the same range of
g,n,m,\overline{d}
by induction on
2g-2+n+m
. The base case
2g-2+n+m=m-1
, which is achieved only for
g=0
and
n=1
, is trivial because we obviously have
B^{m}_{0,d_{1}}=\widetilde{B}^{m}_{0,d_{1}}=\psi_{1}^{d_{1}}
, which is zero because
d_{1}>m-2=\dim{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,m+1}
.

Now suppose
2g-2+n+m>m-1
. We split the sum on the right-hand side of (
3.3
) as follows:

(3.5)

\displaystyle\widetilde{B}^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}:=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c)}_{g,n,m;\circ}\\
l_{0}<\deg(T)\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\deg(T)-1}\mathrm{e}_{*}[T,\overline{d}]+\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c)}_{g,n,m;\circ}\\
l_{0}=\deg(T)\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\deg(T)-1}\mathrm{e}_{*}[T,\overline{d}].

For a tree
T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
, it is clear that if
l_{0}<\deg(T)
, then
2g_{0}-2+|I_{0}|+m<2g-2+n+m
; this follows from the fact that for each
1\leq\l\leq\deg(T)
, there is at least one vertex
v
with
l_{T}(v)=l
that is not potentially unstable. Therefore, by the induction assumption, the first sum on the right-hand side of (
3.5
) is equal to zero. The second sum on the right-hand side of (
3.5
) is obviously equal to
B^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}
, and thus it is equal to zero, as required.
∎

Let us now continue the proof of Theorem
3.4
. Using Proposition
3.6
, we see that it is sufficient to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 3.8
.

For any
n\geq 1
,
g,m\geq 0
, and
\overline{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})\in\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}^{n}
, we have
\widetilde{B}^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}=\mathrm{Coef}_{x_{1}^{d_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{d_{n}}}P_{g,n,m}
.

Proof.

Consider an arbitrary tree
T\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m}
and a function
q\colon\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T)\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}
. For any
h\in H^{e}_{+}(T)
, let us attach
q(h)+1
extra legs to the vertex
v(\iota(h))
. We obtain a tree from
\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,nd)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
, which we denote by
T_{q}
. The definition of the class
\widetilde{B}^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}
can be rewritten in the following way:

\widetilde{B}^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}=\sumop\displaylimits_{T\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m}}(-1)^{|E(T)|}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\colon\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T)\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}\\
q(\sigma_{i})=d_{i}\end{subarray}}\mathrm{e}_{*}\xi_{T_{q}*}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T_{q})}\psi_{h}^{q(h)}\right).

Therefore, we have to prove that

\sumop\displaylimits_{T\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m}}(-1)^{|E(T)|}\sumop\displaylimits_{q\colon\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T)\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}}\mathrm{e}_{*}\xi_{T_{q}*}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T_{q})}\psi_{h}^{q(h)}\right)\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{q(\sigma_{i})}=P_{g,n,m}.

Clearly, this follows from the equation

(3.6)

\sumop\displaylimits_{q\colon\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T)\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}}\mathrm{e}_{*}\xi_{T_{q}*}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T_{q})}\psi_{h}^{q(h)}\right)\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{q(\sigma_{i})}\\
=\sumop\displaylimits_{p\colon\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}}\xi_{T*}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)}\psi_{h}^{p(h)}\right)\prodop\displaylimits_{h\in H_{+}^{e}(T)}x_{I_{h}}^{p(h)+1}\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{p(\sigma_{i})},\quad T\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m},

which we are going to prove by induction on
|V(T)|
.

The base case
|V(T)|=1
is obvious. We proceed to the induction step and assume
|V(T)|\geq 2
. Choose a vertex
v\in V(T)
that does not have direct descendants. Denote by
\widetilde{h}
a unique half-edge from
H^{e}_{-}(T)
incident to
v
, and let
h^{\prime}:=\iota(\widetilde{h})
. Denote by
T^{\prime}
the tree obtained from
T
by deleting the vertex
v
together with all of the half-edges incident to it. We have
T^{\prime}\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g-g(v),n-|I_{h^{\prime}}|+1,m}
, and we have a natural inclusion
\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T^{\prime})\subset\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T)
.

For a function
q\colon\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T)\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}
, set
q^{\prime}:=q|_{\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T^{\prime})}
. We can express the cohomology class in a summand on the left-hand side of (
3.6
) as follows:

\mathrm{e}_{*}\xi_{T_{q}*}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T_{q})}\psi_{h}^{q(h)}\right)=\mathrm{gl}_{*}\left(\mathrm{e}_{*}\xi_{T^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}*}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}})}\psi_{h}^{q^{\prime}(h)}\right)\otimes\pi_{*}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{j\in I_{h^{\prime}}}\psi_{j}^{q(\sigma_{j})}\right)\right),

where
\mathrm{gl}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g-g(v),n-|I_{h^{\prime}}|+1+m}\times{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g(v),|I_{h^{\prime}}|+1}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+m}
is the map given by gluing the marked points corresponding to the half-edges
\widetilde{h}
and
h^{\prime}
, and
\pi\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g(v),|I_{h^{\prime}}|+2+q^{\prime}(h^{\prime})}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g(v),|I_{h^{\prime}}|+1}
is the map forgetting the
q^{\prime}(h^{\prime})+1
marked points corresponding to the extra legs attached to
v\in V(T_{q})
. Therefore, the left-hand side of (
3.6
) is equal to

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{q^{\prime}\colon\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T^{\prime})\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}}\mathrm{gl}_{*}\left(\mathrm{e}_{*}\xi_{T^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}*}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}})}\psi_{h}^{q^{\prime}(h)}\right)\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\llbracket n\rrbracket\backslash I_{h^{\prime}}}x_{i}^{q^{\prime}(\sigma_{i})}\otimes\sumop\displaylimits_{l\colon I_{h^{\prime}}\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}}\pi_{*}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{j\in I_{h^{\prime}}}(\psi_{j}x_{j})^{l(j)}\right)\right).

From the string equation (
2.1
), it follows that

\sumop\displaylimits_{l\colon I_{h^{\prime}}\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}}\pi_{*}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{j\in I_{h^{\prime}}}(\psi_{j}x_{j})^{l(j)}\right)=x_{I_{h^{\prime}}}^{q^{\prime}(h^{\prime})+1}\sumop\displaylimits_{l\colon I_{h^{\prime}}\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}}\prodop\displaylimits_{j\in I_{h^{\prime}}}(\psi_{j}x_{j})^{l(j)},

which allows us to rewrite the previous expression as

\mathrm{gl}_{*}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{q^{\prime}\colon\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T^{\prime})\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}}\mathrm{e}_{*}\xi_{T^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}}*}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}})}\psi_{h}^{q^{\prime}(h)}\right)x_{I_{h^{\prime}}}^{q^{\prime}(h^{\prime})+1}\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\llbracket n\rrbracket\backslash I_{h^{\prime}}}x_{i}^{q^{\prime}(\sigma_{i})}\otimes\sumop\displaylimits_{l\colon I_{h^{\prime}}\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}}\prodop\displaylimits_{j\in I_{h^{\prime}}}(\psi_{j}x_{j})^{l(j)}\right).

Applying the induction assumption to the first factor in the tensor product, we obtain exactly the expression on the right-hand side of (
3.6
), as required.
∎

This concludes the proof of Theorem
3.4
.∎

3.2. Another reformulation for any
\boldsymbol{m\geq 0}

In the case
m\leq 1
, an analog of Theorem
3.4
becomes more subtle. Namely, we no longer can combine conjectural relations and their corollaries as we did in the proof of Theorem
3.4
since the conjectural identities given in Conjectures
2
and
3
for
B^{1}_{g,\overline{d}}
and
B^{0}_{g,\overline{d}}
, respectively, have nontrivial right-hand sides. However, there exists some simplification of the expression for
B^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}
along the same lines that holds for any
m
.

Consider a tree
T\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m}
and a function
p\colon\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T)\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Let
[T,p]
denote the class

\displaystyle[T,p]\coloneqq\xi_{T*}\left(\prodop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)}\psi_{h}^{p(h)}\right)\in R^{|E(T)|+\sumop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)}p(h)}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+m}).

Let us also define two coefficients. One coefficient
C_{\mathrm{lvl}}(T,p)
is a weighted count of possible level structures and depends only on the structure of the tree
T
and the function
p
, and another coefficient
C_{\mathrm{str}}(T,p,\overline{d})
is a combinatorial coefficient that also takes into account a given vector
\overline{d}
(the subscript “
\mathrm{str}
” refers to the fact that it reflects the combinatorics of the string equation).

A level function
l\colon V(T)\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 1}
is called
p
-admissible
if for every
1\leq i<\deg(l)
, we have the following inequality:

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)\\
l(h)\leq i\end{subarray}}p(h)+\left|\left\{\left.h\in H_{+}^{e}(T)\;\right|\;l(h)<i\right\}\right|\leq 2\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in V(T)\\
l(v)\leq i\end{subarray}}g(v)-2+m

(informally, the left-hand side of this expression is just the degree of the class defined by the stable tree obtained from
T
by cutting at the level
i
). Let
\mathcal{L}(T,p)
denote the set of all
p
-admissible level functions on
T
. Define

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{lvl}}(T,p)\coloneqq\sumop\displaylimits_{l\in\mathcal{L}(T,p)}(-1)^{\deg(l)-1}.

For each
h\in\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)
, let
H_{h}\subset\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)
be the subset of all half-edges that are descendants of
h
excluding
h
itself. The combinatorial coefficient
C_{\mathrm{str}}(T,p,\overline{d})
is set to zero (or, alternatively, just undefined) unless
|E(T)|+\sumop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)}p(h)=d_{\llbracket n\rrbracket}
. If that equality holds, then

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{str}}(T,p,\overline{d})\coloneqq\frac{1}{\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}(d_{i}+1)!}\prodop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I_{h}}(d_{i}+1)-\sumop\displaylimits_{h^{\prime}\in H_{h}}(p(h^{\prime})+1)\right)_{(p(h)+1)},

where we recall that
(a)_{(b)}=a(a-1)\cdots(a-b+1)
is the Pochhammer symbol. Note that from this definition,
C_{\mathrm{str}}(T,p,\overline{d})
is equal to zero unless
I_{h}
is nonempty for every
h\in\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)
(or, equivalently, unless each vertex that does not have direct descendants has at least one regular leg attached to it).

Example 3.9
.

Recall Example
3.2
. In this case, the corresponding coefficients are
C_{\mathrm{lvl}}(T,p)=\delta_{p_{3}+p_{4}\leq 2g_{1}}\delta_{p_{1}+p_{3}+p_{4}+p_{5}+1\leq 2(g_{1}+g_{2})}
(here by
\delta
-symbols we denote the functions that take value
1
once the inequality in the subscript is satisfied and
0
otherwise). Note that there is at most one admissible level function
l\in\mathcal{L}(T,p)
, which assigns to a vertex of genus
g_{i}
the value
i
. The coefficient
C_{\mathrm{str}}(T,p,\overline{d})
is equal to

\displaystyle\frac{(d_{2}-p_{2})_{(p_{5}+1)}(d_{1}+d_{2}-p_{1}-p_{2}-p_{5}-1)_{(p_{4}+1)}\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{3}(d_{i}+1)_{(p_{i}+1)}}{\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{3}(d_{i}+1)!}.

A simplified formula for
B^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}
is given by the following theorem.

Theorem 3.10
.

We have

\displaystyle B^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}=\sumop\displaylimits_{T\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\colon\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\\
|E(T)|+\sumop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)}p(h)=d_{\llbracket n\rrbracket}\end{subarray}}C_{\mathrm{lvl}}(T,p)C_{\mathrm{str}}(T,p,\overline{d})\cdot[T,p].

Proof.

The proof follows essentially the same ideas as some steps of the proof of Theorem
3.4
. However, we arrange it a bit differently to stress the origin of the coefficients
C_{\mathrm{lvl}}(T,p)
and
C_{\mathrm{str}}(T,p,\overline{d})
. In a nutshell, we just carefully describe in steps the pushfoward
\mathrm{e}_{*}
in the definition of the class
B^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}
.

Recall Definition
2.1
. Our goal is to explicitly compute
\mathrm{e}_{*}[T,\overline{d}]
for any
T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c,a)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
. We perform the pushforward in two steps. In the first step, we formally apply the string equation given by Equation (
2.1
) to the vertices of the graph
T
. To this end, in order to efficiently treat the case of potentially unstable vertices, we introduce an extra convention that extends the string equation.
In the string equation (
2.1
), one has to assume
2g-2+k>0
and
p_{i}\geq 0
,
i=1,\ldots,k
. It is convenient to formally extend the range of applications of the string equation. We consider the case
g=0
,
k=2
, and
q\geq 0
, and we formally set

(3.7)

\displaystyle\pi_{*}(\psi_{1}^{q}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,q+3}})=\psi_{1}^{-1}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,2}}

for the map
\pi\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,q+3}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,2}
that forgets the last
q+1
marked points. This map has to be understood formally: we are going to apply
\mathrm{e}_{*}
to the graphs in
\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c,a)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
, and Equation (
3.7
) just means that at an intermediate step of the computation, we will use unstable vertices of genus
0
with two incident half-edges (these vertices will disappear in the final formula for the pushforward). To make this precise, let us introduce auxiliary definitions.

We consider
rooted trees
in
\mathrm{RT}_{g,n,m}
, which is an extension of
\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m}
where we allow trees to have unstable vertices of type
(0,2)
; that is, we allow vertices
v
of genus
g(v)=0
with just two incident half-edges, one in
\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)
and one in the direction of the mother of
v
. The root cannot be unstable, however. Denote by
\mathrm{DRT}_{g,n,m}
the set of pairs
(T,p)
, where
T\in\mathrm{RT}_{g,n,m}
and
p\colon\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}
is a function such that
p^{-1}(-1)
is exactly the subset of
\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)
attached to unstable vertices. Let us call such pairs
decorated rooted trees
.

In the same way as for stable rooted trees, for
T\in\mathrm{RT}_{g,n,m}
, we consider level functions
l\colon V(T)\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
and the canonical level function
l_{T}\colon V(T)\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 1}
. We say that a rooted tree in
\mathrm{RT}_{g,n,m}
is
complete
if the canonical level function satisfies exactly the same conditions as the canonical level function of a complete balanced stable rooted tree in
\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m;\circ}^{(b,c)}
(
cf.
Section
2.2
) with the condition “
for every
1\leq l\leq\deg(T)
, the set of vertices
l_{T}^{-1}(l)
contains at least one vertex that is not potentially unstable
” replaced by “
for every
1\leq l\leq\deg(T)
, the set of vertices
l_{T}^{-1}(l)
contains at least one vertex that is not unstable
.” Let
\mathrm{RT}^{(c)}_{g,n,m}
denote the subset of complete rooted trees. Denote by
\mathrm{DRT}^{(c)}_{g,n,m}\subset\mathrm{DRT}_{g,n,m}
the subset of pairs
(T,p)
where
T\in\mathrm{RT}^{(c)}_{g,n,m}
. Let
\mathrm{DRT}^{(c,a)}_{g,n,m}\subset\mathrm{DRT}^{(c)}_{g,n,m}
be the set of so-called
admissible
complete decorated rooted trees
(T,p)
satisfying the additional system of inequalities

(3.8)

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in H^{e}_{+}(T)\\
l_{T}(h)\leq k\end{subarray}}p(h)+\left|\left\{\left.h\in H^{e}_{+}(T)\;\right|\;l_{T}(h)<k\right\}\right|\leq 2g_{k}(T)-2+m,\quad\text{for any }1\leq k<\deg(T).

We can now proceed to the description of the first step in our computation of the pushforward
\mathrm{e}_{*}[T,\overline{d}]
,
T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c,a)}_{g,n,m;\circ}
. We construct a map
f_{1}\colon\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m;\circ}^{(b,c,a)}\times\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}\left<\mathrm{DRT}^{(c)}_{g,n,m}\right>
as follows.

•

For a pair
(T,\overline{d})\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m;\circ}^{(b,c,a)}\times\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}^{n}
, consider a potentially unstable vertex
v
. There is exactly one half-edge in
\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)
attached to
v
; denote it by
h^{\prime}
, and denote by
\widetilde{h}
the half-edge attached to
v
that is directed to the mother of
v
. If
q(h^{\prime})=q(\iota(\widetilde{h}))
, then we replace
v
with an unstable genus
0
vertex that retains just the two half-edges
h^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{h}
(forgetting all of the extra legs attached to
v
), and we set
p(h^{\prime}):=-1
. We do this for each potentially unstable vertex
v
. If there is at least one potentially unstable vertex
v
with
q(h^{\prime})\neq q(\iota(\widetilde{h}))
, then we set
f_{1}(T,\overline{d}):=0
(note that if
q(h^{\prime})\neq q(\iota(\widetilde{h}))
, then
\mathrm{e}_{*}[T,\overline{d}]=0
).

•

Consider a not potentially unstable vertex
v
of
T
that also does not coincide with the root. Let
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}
be the half-edges in
\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)
attached to
v
, and denote by
\widetilde{h}
the half-edge attached to
v
that is directed to the mother of
v
. We replace
v
with a vertex of the same genus that retains just the half-edges
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k},\widetilde{h}
and take the weighted sum over all choices of
p(h_{1}),\ldots,p(h_{k})
such that
\sumop\displaylimits q(h_{i})-\sumop\displaylimits p(h_{i})=q(\iota(\widetilde{h}))+1
with the weights equal to the coefficients on the right-hand side of Equation (
2.1
). We do this for each vertex
v
that is not potentially unstable and that is not the root.

•

For the root of
T
, we just define
p(h):=q(h)
for any half-edge
h\in\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T)
attached to it.

Clearly, as the outcome of the above procedure, we obtain a linear combination of decorated rooted trees from
\mathrm{DRT}^{(c)}_{g,n,m}
. We take this linear combination as the value of the function
f_{1}
on the pair
(T,\overline{d})
.

We claim that

(3.9)

\displaystyle f_{1}(T,\overline{d})=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}(T^{\prime},p)\in\mathrm{DRT}^{(c)}_{g,n,m}\\
(T^{\prime},p)\sim(T,\overline{d})\end{subarray}}\frac{\prodop\displaylimits_{h\in\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in I_{h}}(d_{i}+1)-\sumop\displaylimits_{h^{\prime}\in H_{h}}(p(h^{\prime})+1)\right)_{\left(p(h)+1\right)}}{\prodop\displaylimits_{i=1}^{n}(d_{i}+1)!}(T^{\prime},p),

where the notation
(T^{\prime},p)\sim(T,\overline{d})
means that
(T^{\prime},p)
is obtained from
(T,\overline{d})
by removing all extra legs and making a choice of the values of the function
p
according to the construction of the map
f_{1}
. Indeed, the coefficient of
(T^{\prime},p)
on the right-hand side of (
3.9
) is equal to the product of coefficients prescribed by choices of summands made according to Equation (
2.1
): we just rewrite the product of these coefficients in terms of the vector
\overline{d}
and the function
p
on
\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T^{\prime})
.

Moreover, several remarks are in order:

•

It follows from the string equations that Equation (
2.3
) for
T
implies Equation (
3.8
) for every
(T^{\prime},p)\sim(T,\overline{d})
; thus
(T^{\prime},p)
belongs to
\mathrm{DRT}^{(c,a)}_{g,n,m}
. Therefore, the image of
f_{1}
belongs to
{\mathbb Q}\left<\mathrm{DRT}^{(c,a)}_{g,n,m}\right>
.

•

It also follows from the string equations that

(3.10)

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T^{\prime})\end{subarray}}p(h)+|E(T^{\prime})|=d_{\llbracket n\rrbracket}.

•

The function
q
on
\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T)
is uniquely reconstructed from
\overline{d}
and the function
p
on
\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T^{\prime})
, but, given arbitrary
(T^{\prime},p)\in\mathrm{DRT}^{(c,a)}_{g,n,m}
and
\overline{d}\in\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}^{n}
, such a function
q
may not exist. Note however that if condition (
3.10
) is satisfied, then such a function
q
exists if and only if the coefficient of
(T^{\prime},p)
on the right-hand side of (
3.9
) is nonzero.

•

The vertices of
T
and
T^{\prime}
are in a natural bijection, and the canonical level functions for both trees are identified by this bijection. In particular,
\deg(T^{\prime})=\deg(T)
.

In the second step, we contract all unstable vertices of a decorated rooted tree
(T^{\prime},p)\in\mathrm{DRT}^{(c,a)}_{g,n,m}
. This defines a map
f_{2}\colon\mathrm{DRT}^{(c,a)}_{g,n,m}\to\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m}
,
f_{2}\colon(T^{\prime},p)\mapsto T^{\prime\prime}
, where the functions
p
and
l_{T^{\prime}}
defined for
T^{\prime}
naturally descend to functions
p\colon\widetilde{H}_{+}^{em}(T^{\prime\prime})\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
l\colon V(T^{\prime\prime})\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
(all values of
p
are now nonnegative since there are no unstable vertices left). The resulting level function
l\colon V(T^{\prime\prime})\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
is obviously
p
-admissible. Also, given
\overline{d}\in\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}^{n}
, condition (
3.10
) is equivalent to the condition
\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T^{\prime\prime})\end{subarray}}p(h)+|E(T^{\prime\prime})|=d_{\llbracket n\rrbracket}
. Conversely, given
T^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m}
,
p\colon V(T^{\prime\prime})\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}
, and
l\in\mathcal{L}(T^{\prime\prime},p)
, there is a unique
(T^{\prime},p)\in\mathrm{DRT}^{(c,a)}_{g,n,m}
such that
f_{2}(T^{\prime},p)=T^{\prime\prime}
and
p
and
l
on
T^{\prime\prime}
are induced by the corresponding functions
p
and
l_{T^{\prime}}
on
T^{\prime}
(with
\deg(T^{\prime})=\deg(l)
). Finally, note that, also given
\overline{d}\in\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}^{n}
, the coefficient of
(T^{\prime},p)
in Equation (
3.9
) is equal to
C_{\mathrm{str}}(T^{\prime\prime},p,\overline{d})
.

This implies that for any
(T,\overline{d})\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m;\circ}^{(b,c,a)}\times\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}^{n}
, the class
e_{*}[T,\overline{d}]
is equal to

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}_{*}[T,\overline{d}]=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}(T^{\prime},p)\in\mathrm{DRT}^{(c,a)}_{g,n,m}\\
(T^{\prime},p)\sim(T,\overline{d})\end{subarray}}C_{\mathrm{str}}\left(f_{2}(T^{\prime},p),p,\overline{d}\right)\left[f_{2}(T^{\prime},p),p\right],

and therefore

\displaystyle B^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c,a)}_{g,n,m;\circ}}(-1)^{\deg(T)-1}\mathrm{e}_{*}[T,\overline{d}]

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{T\in\mathrm{SRT}^{(b,c,a)}_{g,n,m;\circ}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}(T^{\prime},p)\in\mathrm{DRT}^{(c,a)}_{g,n,m}\\
(T^{\prime},p)\sim(T,\overline{d})\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\deg(l_{T^{\prime}})-1}C_{\mathrm{str}}\left(f_{2}(T^{\prime},p),p,\overline{d}\right)\left[f_{2}(T^{\prime},p),p\right]

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}(T^{\prime},p)\in\mathrm{DRT}^{(c,a)}_{g,n,m}\\
\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T^{\prime})\end{subarray}}p(h)+|E(T^{\prime})|=d_{\llbracket n\rrbracket}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\deg(l_{T^{\prime}})-1}C_{\mathrm{str}}\left(f_{2}(T^{\prime},p),p,\overline{d}\right)\left[f_{2}(T^{\prime},p),p\right]

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{T^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{SRT}_{g,n,m}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\colon\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T^{\prime\prime})\to\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}\\
\sumop\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in\widetilde{H}^{em}_{+}(T^{\prime\prime})\end{subarray}}p(h)+|E(T^{\prime\prime})|=d_{\llbracket n\rrbracket}\end{subarray}}C_{\mathrm{str}}\left(T^{\prime\prime},p,\overline{d}\right)\left[T^{\prime\prime},p\right]\cdot\sumop\displaylimits_{l\in\mathcal{L}(T^{\prime\prime},p)}(-1)^{\deg(l)-1},

which proves the theorem.
∎

4. Conjectural relations and the fundamental properties of the DR and the DZ hierarchies

In this section, we show how certain fundamental properties of the Dubrovin–Zhang (DZ) and the double ramification (DR) hierarchies associated to F-cohomological field theories (F-CohFTs) on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
naturally follow from our Conjectures
1
and
2
. This extends the results from
[
BGR19
]
, where the authors showed that Conjecture
3
naturally implies that the DZ and the DR hierarchies associated to an arbitrary CohFT are Miura equivalent. Actually, in all of the aspects of the theory of integrable systems associated to CohFTs that we consider in this paper, a CohFT can be replaced by a slightly more general object called a
partial CohFT
, introduced in
[
LRZ15
]
. In the theory of the DR hierarchies, it was first noticed in
[
BDGR18
, Section 9.1]
. So we will systematically work with partial CohFTs instead of CohFTs.

We also discuss the role of the classes
B^{m}_{g,\overline{d}}
with
\sumop\displaylimits d_{i}=2g-2+m
, which are not involved in the conjectural relations from Conjectures
1
,
2
, and
3
. It occurs that these classes control the polynomial parts of the equations (which are conjecturally polynomial) of the DZ hierarchies and the Miura transformation that conjecturally relates the DZ and the DR hierarchies.

4.1. Differential polynomials and evolutionary PDEs

Let us fix
N\geq 1
and consider formal variables
w^{1},\ldots,w^{N}
. Let us briefly recall main notions and notation in the formal theory of evolutionary PDEs with one spatial variable (and refer a reader, for example, to
[
BRS21
]
for details):

•

To the formal variables
w^{\alpha}
, we attach formal variables
w^{\alpha}_{d}
with
d\geq 0
, and we introduce the ring of
differential polynomials

\mathcal{A}_{w}:=\mathbb C[[w^{*}]][w^{*}_{\geq 1}]
(in
[
BRS21
]
, it is denoted by
\mathcal{A}^{0}_{w}
). We identify
w^{\alpha}_{0}=w^{\alpha}
and also set
w^{\alpha}_{x}:=w^{\alpha}_{1}
,
w^{\alpha}_{xx}:=w^{\alpha}_{2}
, ….

•

The operator
{\partial}_{x}\colon\mathcal{A}_{w}\to\mathcal{A}_{w}
is defined by
{\partial}_{x}:=\sumop\displaylimits_{d\geq 0}w^{\alpha}_{d+1}\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}w^{\alpha}_{d}}
.

•

Let
\mathcal{A}_{w;d}\subset\mathcal{A}_{w}
be the homogeneous component of (differential) degree
d
, where
\deg w^{\alpha}_{i}:=i
. For
f\in\mathcal{A}_{w}
, we denote by
f^{[d]}\in\mathcal{A}_{w;d}
the image of
f
under the canonical projection
\mathcal{A}_{w}\to\mathcal{A}_{w;d}
. We will also use the notation
\mathcal{A}_{w;\leq d}:=\bigoplusop\displaylimits_{i=0}^{d}\mathcal{A}_{w;i}
.

•

The extended space of differential polynomials is defined by
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{w}:=\mathcal{A}_{w}[[\varepsilon]]
. Let
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{w;k}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{w}
be the homogeneous component of degree
k
, where
\deg\varepsilon:=-1
.

•

A
Miura transformation
(that is close to the identity) is a change of variables
w^{\alpha}\mapsto\widetilde{w}^{\alpha}(w^{*}_{*},\varepsilon)
of the form
\widetilde{w}^{\alpha}(w^{*}_{*},\varepsilon)=w^{\alpha}+\varepsilon f^{\alpha}(w^{*}_{*},\varepsilon)
, where

f^{\alpha}\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{w;1}
.

•

A system of
evolutionary PDEs
(with one spatial variable) is a system of equations of the form
\frac{{\partial}w^{\alpha}}{{\partial}t}=P^{\alpha}
,
1\leq\alpha\leq N
, where
P^{\alpha}\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{w}
. Two systems
\frac{{\partial}w^{\alpha}}{{\partial}t}=P^{\alpha}
and
\frac{{\partial}w^{\alpha}}{{\partial}s}=Q^{\alpha}
are said to be
compatible
(or, equivalently, the flows
\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}t}
and
\frac{{\partial}}{{\partial}s}
are said to
commute
) if
\sumop\displaylimits_{n\geq 0}\left(\frac{{\partial}P^{\alpha}}{{\partial}w^{\beta}_{n}}{\partial}_{x}^{n}Q^{\beta}-\frac{{\partial}Q^{\alpha}}{{\partial}w^{\beta}_{n}}{\partial}_{x}^{n}P^{\beta}\right)=0
for any
1\leq\alpha\leq N
.

4.2. F-CohFTs and partial CohFTs

4.2.1. Definitions

Definition 4.1

(
cf.

[
BR21
]
)
.

An
F-cohomological field theory
(F-CohFT) is a system of linear maps

c_{g,n+1}\colon V^{*}\otimes V^{\otimes n}\longrightarrow H^{\mathrm{even}}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+1}),\quad g,n\geq 0,\,\,2g-1+n>0,

where
V
is an arbitrary finite-dimensional vector space, together with a special element
e\in V
, called the
unit
, such that, choosing a basis
e_{1},\ldots,e_{\dim V}
of
V
and the dual basis
e^{1},\ldots,e^{\dim V}
of
V^{*}
, the following axioms are satisfied:

(i)

The maps
c_{g,n+1}
are equivariant with respect to the
S_{n}
-action permuting the
n
copies of
V
in
V^{*}\otimes V^{\otimes n}
and the last
n
marked points in
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+1}
, respectively.

(ii)

We have
\pi^{*}c_{g,n+1}(e^{\alpha_{0}}\otimes\otimes_{i=1}^{n}e_{\alpha_{i}})=c_{g,n+2}(e^{\alpha_{0}}\otimes\otimes_{i=1}^{n}e_{\alpha_{i}}\otimes e)
for
1\leq\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n}\leq\dim V
, where
\pi\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+2}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n+1}
is the map that forgets the last marked point. Moreover,
c_{0,3}(e^{\alpha}\otimes e_{\beta}\otimes e)=\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}
for
1\leq\alpha,\beta\leq\dim V
.

(iii)

We have
